    that \( \epsilon \in A \) and \( \orderedpair{\epsilon}{\zeta} \in g \) are both true.
    Since \( g \in \mleft\{ f, g \mright\} \), it follows that \( \orderedpair{\epsilon}{\zeta} \in h \).
    In other words, \( \epsilon \in A \) and \( \orderedpair{\epsilon}{\zeta} \in h \) are necessarily true.
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-restricted-function}, we conclude that \( \orderedpair{\epsilon}{\zeta} \in \restrictedfunctionsmall{h}{A} \)
    and equivalently \( q \in \restrictedfunctionsmall{h}{A} \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    It is clear that \( q \in \restrictedfunctionsmall{h}{A} \) is implied in both cases. From this, we conclude that
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{if \( q \in \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \union \, \restrictedfunctionsmall{g}{A} \), then \( q \in \restrictedfunctionsmall{h}{A} \)}
    \end{equation*}
    Since \( q \) is arbitrary, we deduce from \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization,dfn:metatheory-subsets-and-proper-subsets} that
    \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \union \, \restrictedfunctionsmall{g}{A} \subseteq \restrictedfunctionsmall{h}{A} \).
    Having shown that \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{h}{A} \) and \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \union \, \restrictedfunctionsmall{g}{A} \) are subsets of each
    other, we conclude from \cref{thm:metatheory-equality-in-terms-of-subsets} that
    \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{h}{A} = \restrictedfunctionsmall{f}{A} \union \, \restrictedfunctionsmall{g}{A} \) as required.
\end{proof}

\begin{dfn}[Inverse]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-inverse-of-a-function}
    If \( f \) is a function, then the \keyword{inverse} of \( f \) is the set given by
    \begin{equation*}
        f^{-1} = \setbuilderbig{\orderedpair{y}{x}}{\orderedpair{x}{y} \in f}
    \end{equation*}
\end{dfn}

\begin{rem}
    Note that \( f^{-1} \) is not necessarily a function.
\end{rem}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-existence-of-inverse-of-a-function}
    For any function \( f \), \( f^{-1} \) exists.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Since \( f \) is a function, \cref{thm:metatheory-existence-of-domain-and-range-of-function} guarantees the existence of \( \dom f \) and \( \ran f \).
    Furthermore, \cref{thm:metatheory-existence-of-cartesian-products} guarantees the existence of sets \( \dom f \times \ran f \) and \( \ran f \times \dom f \).
    Now let \( \varphi(x) \) be the predicate defined as follows:
    \begin{equation*}
        \varphi(x) \coloneq \text{there exists an \( a \) and a \( b \) such that \( x = \orderedpair{b}{a} \) and \( \orderedpair{a}{b} \in f \)}
    \end{equation*}
    Let \( p \) be an arbitrary object.
    Suppose that \( \varphi(p) \) is a true statement;
    that is, there exists an \( \alpha \) and a \( \beta \) such that \( p = \orderedpair{\beta}{\alpha} \) and \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{\beta} \in f \).
    Since \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{\beta} \in f \), it follows from \cref{dfn:metatheory-domain-of-a-function,dfn:metatheory-range-of-a-function} that
    \( \alpha \in \dom f \) and \( \beta \in \ran f \).
    Clearly, \( \orderedpair{\beta}{\alpha} \in \ran f \times \dom f \) by \cref{dfn:metatheory-cartesian-product}.
    Thus, \( p \in \ran f \times \dom f \).
    Since \( p \) is arbitrary, we have by \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization}
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( \varphi(x) \) implies \( x \in \ran f \times \dom f \)}
    \end{equation*}
    By \cref{thm:metatheory-existence-of-unrestricted-comprehension-set},
    the set \( \setbuilder{x}{\varphi(x)} = \setbuilder{\orderedpair{y}{x}}{\orderedpair{x}{y} \in f} = f^{-1} \) exists.
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-if-a-function-is-injective-then-inverse-is-a-function}
    Let \( A \), \( B \), and \( f \) be sets.
    If \( \injection{f}{A}{B} \), then \( \bijection{f^{-1}}{\imageSet{f}{A}}{A} \)
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    To prove that \( \bijection{f^{-1}}{\imageSet{f}{A}}{A} \), we must establish the following claims:
    \begin{enumerate*}[label=(\roman*)]
        \item \( \function{f^{-1}}{\imageSet{f}{A}}{A} \), \phantomsection \label{enum:metatheory-inverse-of-a-function-claim1}
        \item \( f^{-1} \) is injective, \phantomsection \label{enum:metatheory-inverse-of-a-function-claim2}
        \item \( \ran f^{-1} = A \). \phantomsection \label{enum:metatheory-inverse-of-a-function-claim3}
    \end{enumerate*}

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    {\scshape Claim} \ref{enum:metatheory-inverse-of-a-function-claim1}:
    We must show that \( f^{-1} \) satisfies the two conditions given in \cref{dfn:metatheory-functions-in-terms-of-two-sets}.
    Let \( p \) denote an arbitrary object.
    Suppose that \( p \in f^{-1} \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-inverse-of-a-function}, it follows that \( p = \orderedpair{b}{a} \) for some \( a \) and \( b \)
    and that \( \orderedpair{a}{b} \in f \).
    Furthermore, \cref{dfn:metatheory-injection} also requires that \( \function{f}{A}{B} \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-functions-in-terms-of-two-sets}, it follows that \( f \subseteq A \times B \).
    Since \( \orderedpair{a}{b} \in f \), \( a \in A \) must be true by \cref{dfn:metatheory-cartesian-product}.
    Also, \( b = f(a) \) by \cref{dfn:metateory-value-of-a-function}.
    Since \( b = f(a) \) and \( a \in A \), it follows from \cref{dfn:metatheory-image-of-a-set-under-a-function}
    that \( b \in \imageSet{f}{A} \).
    Since \( b \in \imageSet{f}{A} \), \( a \in A \), and \( \orderedpair{b}{a} \in f^{-1} \) are all true,
    it follows from \cref{dfn:metatheory-cartesian-product} that \( \orderedpair{b}{a} \in \imageSet{f}{A} \times A \).
    In other words, \( p \in f^{-1} \) implies \( p \in \imageSet{f}{A} \times A \) for any arbitrary \( p \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-subsets-and-proper-subsets}, we have
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:f-inverse-satisfies-first-condition}
        f^{-1} \subseteq \imageSet{f}{A} \times A
    \end{equation}
    Now let \( c \) be an arbitrary object.
    Suppose that \( c \in \imageSet{f}{A} \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-image-of-a-set-under-a-function}, there must exist some \( d \in A \)
    such that \( \orderedpair{d}{c} \in f \).
    But if \( \orderedpair{d}{c} \in f \), then necessarily \( \orderedpair{c}{d} \in f^{-1} \) by \cref{dfn:metatheory-inverse-of-a-function}.
    We must now prove that this \( d \) is \aref{dfn:metatheory-unique-satisfaction}{unique}.
    Suppose that \( e \) is another element of \( A \) such that \( \orderedpair{e}{c} \in f \).
    This would mean that \( f(d) = c = f(e) \).
    But \( f \) is injective, so \( d \) and \( e \) must be equal by \cref{dfn:metatheory-injection}.
    Since \( c \) is arbitrary, we infer from \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} that
    \begin{equation}
        \phantomsection \label{eq:f-inverse-satisfies-second-condition}
        \text{for all \( x \in \imageSet{f}{A} \), there exists a unique \( y \in A \) such that \( \orderedpair{x}{y} \in f^{-1} \)}
    \end{equation}
    It should be obvious from \eqref{eq:f-inverse-satisfies-first-condition} and \eqref{eq:f-inverse-satisfies-second-condition} that
    \( f^{-1} \) meets the two conditions stated in \cref{dfn:metatheory-functions-in-terms-of-two-sets}.
    Therefore, \( \function{f^{-1}}{\imageSet{f}{A}}{A} \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    {\scshape Claim} \ref{enum:metatheory-inverse-of-a-function-claim2}:
    Suppose that \( f^{-1}(a) = f^{-1}{b} \) for some arbitrary \( a \) and \( b \);
    that is, there exists some \( c \) such that \( \orderedpair{a}{c} \in f^{-1} \) and \( \orderedpair{b}{c} \in f^{-1} \)
    are both true.
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-inverse-of-a-function}, it follows that \( \orderedpair{c}{a} \in f \) and \( \orderedpair{c}{b} \in f \)
    are both true.
    But \( f \) is a function, so \( a \) and \( b \) must be equal by \cref{dfn:metatheory-function}.
    In other words,
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{if \( f^{-1}(a) = f^{-1}(b) \), then \( a = b \)}
    \end{equation*}
    Thus, \( f^{-1} \) must be injective by \cref{dfn:metatheory-injection}.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    {\scshape Claim} \ref{enum:metatheory-inverse-of-a-function-claim3}:
    The first claim itself already implies that \( \ran f^{-1} \subseteq A \).
    It remains to prove that \( A \subseteq \ran f^{-1} \).
    Let \( a \in A \) be arbitrary.
    Since \( \function{f}{A}{B} \), it follows that \( \dom f = A \).
    Thus, \( a \in \dom f \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-domain-of-a-function}, there must exist a \( b \) such that
    \( \orderedpair{a}{b} \in f \), which in turn implies that \( \orderedpair{b}{a} \in f^{-1} \).
    In other words,
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{if \( a \in A \), then there exists a \( b \) such that \( \orderedpair{b}{a} \in f^{-1} \)}
    \end{equation*}
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-range-of-a-function}, necessarily \( a \in \ran f^{-1} \).
    Therefore,
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{if \( a \in A \), then \( a \in \ran f^{-1} \)}
    \end{equation*}
    Since \( a \) denotes an arbitrary element of \( A \), we have by \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization}
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( x \in A \) implies \( x \in \ran f^{-1} \) }
    \end{equation*}
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-subsets-and-proper-subsets}, \( A \subseteq \ran f^{-1} \) as required.
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-inverse-of-bijection-is-another-bijection}
    Let \( A \), \( B \), and \( f \) be sets.
    If \( \bijection{f}{A}{B} \), then \( \bijection{f^{-1}}{B}{A} \)
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Since \( \bijection{f}{A}{B} \), it follows that \aref{dfn:metatheory-bijection}{\( \injection{f}{A}{B} \) and \( \surjection{f}{A}{B} \) are both true}.
    Since \( \injection{f}{A}{B} \), it follows from \cref{thm:metatheory-if-a-function-is-injective-then-inverse-is-a-function} that
    \( \function{f^{-1}}{\imageSet{f}{A}}{A} \).
    Also, given that \( \surjection{f}{A}{B} \), it follows that \( \ran f = B \).
    But \( \dom f = A \), so by \cref{thm:metatheory-image-of-the-domain-of-a-function-under-the-function-equals-the-range-of-the-function},
    \( \imageSet{f}{A} = \imageSet{f}{\dom f} = \ran f = B \).
    \begin{equation*}
        \therefore \, \function{f^{-1}}{B}{A}
    \end{equation*}
    It remains to prove that \( f^{-1} \) is injective and that \( \ran f^{-1} = A \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Let \( a \), \( b \), and \( c \) be objects in the universe such that \( \orderedpair{a}{c} \in f^{-1} \) and \( \orderedpair{b}{c} \in f^{-1} \)
    are both true.
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-inverse-of-a-function}, it follows that \( \orderedpair{c}{a} \in f \) and \( \orderedpair{c}{b} \in f \).
    Since \( f \) is a function, necessarily \( a \) and \( b \) are equal.
    \begin{equation*}
        \therefore \, \injection{f^{-1}}{B}{A}
    \end{equation*}

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Finally, we prove that \( \ran f^{-1} = A \) by showing that \( A \subseteq \ran f^{-1} \) because \( \ran f^{-1} \subseteq A \) is already implied by
    \( \function{f^{-1}}{B}{A} \).
    Let \( \alpha \) be an arbitrary element of \( A \). Since \( A = \dom f \), it follows that \( \alpha \in \dom f \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-domain-of-a-function}, there must exist a \( \beta \) such that \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{\beta} \in f \).
    But if \( \orderedpair{\alpha}{\beta} \in f \) is true, then necessarily \( \orderedpair{\beta}{\alpha} \in f^{-1} \) by \cref{dfn:metatheory-inverse-of-a-function}.
    Since \( \orderedpair{\beta}{\alpha} \in f^{-1} \) and \( \beta \) is an existing object, necessarily \( \alpha \in \ran f^{-1} \) by
    \cref{dfn:metatheory-range-of-a-function}.
    Thus, \( A \subseteq \ran f^{-1} \) as required.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The Counting Numbers}
Our metatheory is now sophisticated enough that we can build the counting numbers with it.
We will preface the construction of the counting numbers with the following definitions and axiom.

\begin{dfn}[Successor]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-successor}
    For any set \( x \), the set \( x \union \mleft\{ x \mright\} \) is called the \keyword{successor} of \( x \).
\end{dfn}

\begin{dfn}[Inductive Set]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-inductive-set}
    A set \( I \) is \keyword{inductive} iff the following statements are true:
    \begin{enumerate}[topsep=2pt, label=\textup{\texttt{(\alph*)}}]
        \item \( \emptyset \in I \)
        \item for all \( x \), \( x \in I \) implies \( x \union \mleft\{ x \mright\} \in I \)
    \end{enumerate}
\end{dfn}

\begin{meta-axiom}
    \phantomsection \label{meta-axiom:existence-of-inductive-set}
    An inductive set exists in the universe.
\end{meta-axiom}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-existence-of-the-set-of-natural-numbers}
    The set \( \setbuilder{x}{\text{for all \( I \), if \( I \) is an inductive set, then \( x \in I \)}} \) exists.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    We start by defining the following predicate.
    \begin{equation*}
        \varphi(x) \coloneq \ \text{for all \( I \), if \( I \) is an inductive set, then \( x \in I \)}
    \end{equation*}
    \cref{meta-axiom:existence-of-inductive-set} guarantees that there exists at least one inductive set, which we will denote with \( \mathcal{I} \).
    Let \( \alpha \) be an arbitrary object such that \( \varphi(\alpha) \) is a true statement; that is,
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( I \), if \( I \) is an inductive set, then \( \alpha \in I \)}
    \end{equation*}
    Since the set \( \mathcal{I} \) is inductive, it follows that \( \alpha \in \mathcal{I} \).
    In other words, \( \varphi(\alpha) \) implies \( \alpha \in \mathcal{I} \).
    But \( \alpha \) is arbitrary, so we have by \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization}
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( \varphi(x) \) implies \( x \in \mathcal{I} \)}
    \end{equation*}
    Since \( \mathcal{I} \) is an existing set, it follows readilly from \cref{thm:metatheory-existence-of-unrestricted-comprehension-set} that the set
    \( \setbuilder{x}{\varphi(x)} \) exists and is unique.
\end{proof}

\begin{dfn}
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-the-set-of-all-natural-numbers}
    \( \naturalset = \setbuilder{x}{\text{for all \( I \), if \( I \) is an inductive set, then \( x \in I \)}} \)
\end{dfn}

\begin{notation}
    If \( n \in \naturalset \), then we denote the successor of \( n \) as \( n + 1 \).
\end{notation}

\begin{dfn}
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-ordering-of-natural-numbers}
    For any two natural numbers \( m \) and \( n \), we say that \( m \) is \keyword{less than} \( n \) (denoted as \( m < n \))
    iff \( m \in n \).
\end{dfn}

\begin{rem}
    It is not difficult to see that the set \( \naturalset \) contains exactly the counting numbers
    as long as we interpret the symbols for the counting numbers with the following scheme:
    \begin{align*}
        0 &\coloneq \emptyset = \mleft\{ \mright\} \\
        1 &\coloneq 0 \union \mleft\{ 0 \mright\} = \{0\} \\
        2 &\coloneq 1 \union \mleft\{ 1 \mright\} = \{0\} \union \{1\} = \{0,1\}\\
        3 &\coloneq 2 \union \mleft\{ 2 \mright\} = \{0,1\} \union \{2\} = \{0,1,2\}\\
        \vdots
    \end{align*}
    This scheme (courtesy of John von Neumann) essentially defines a natural number as a set that contains every other natural number less than it.
\end{rem}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-the-set-of-natural-numbers-is-a-subset-of-any-inductive-set}
    If \( I \) is an inductive set, then \( \naturalset \subseteq I \)
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( I \) be any inductive set.
    Let \( \alpha \) denote an arbitrary object.
    Suppose that \( \alpha \in \naturalset \). By \cref{dfn:metatheory-the-set-of-all-natural-numbers}, the following statement is true:
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( y \), if \( y \) is an inductive set, then \( \alpha \in y \) }
    \end{equation*}
    Since \( I \) is an object in the universe, \cref{meta-rule:universal-instantiation} yields
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{if \( I \) is inductive, then \( \alpha \in I \)}
    \end{equation*}
    But \( I \) is indeed inductive, so necessarily \( \alpha \in I \) by \cref{meta-rule:modus-ponens}.
    In other words, we have proven that \( \alpha \in \naturalset \) implies \( \alpha \in I \).
    Since \( \alpha \) is arbitary, we conclude by \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} that
    for all \( x \), \( x \in \naturalset \) implies \( x \in I \).
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-subsets-and-proper-subsets}, it follows that \( \naturalset \subseteq I \).
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-the-set-of-all-natural-numbers-is-inductive}
    \( \naturalset \) is an inductive set.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( I \) denote an arbitrary set.
    Suppose that \( I \) is \aref{dfn:metatheory-inductive-set}{inductive}.
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-inductive-set}, necessarily \( \emptyset \in I \).
    In other words, the implication ``if \( I \) is inductive, then \( \emptyset \in I \)'' is true.
    Since \( I \) is arbitrary, it follows from \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization,dfn:metatheory-the-set-of-all-natural-numbers}
    that \( \emptyset \in \naturalset \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Next, let \( \alpha \) denote an arbitrary object. Suppose that \( \alpha \in \naturalset \); that is,
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( I \), if \( I \) is inductive, then \( \alpha \in I \)}
    \end{equation*}
    Let \( I^{\prime} \) denote an arbitary inductive set. By \cref{meta-rule:universal-instantiation}, it follows that
    \( \alpha \in I^{\prime} \). Since \( I^{\prime} \) is inductive and \( \alpha \in I^{\prime} \),
    \cref{dfn:metatheory-inductive-set} guarantees that \( \alpha \union \mleft\{ \alpha \mright\} \in I^{\prime} \).
    Since \( I^{\prime} \) is arbitrary, we apply \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} to infer
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( I \), if \( I \) is inductive, then \( \alpha \union \mleft\{ \alpha \mright\} \in I \)}
    \end{equation*}
    But by \cref{dfn:metatheory-the-set-of-all-natural-numbers}, this implies \( \alpha \union \mleft\{ \alpha \mright\} \in \naturalset \).
    In other words, \( \alpha \in \naturalset \) implies \( \alpha \union \mleft\{ \alpha \mright\} \in \naturalset \).
    Since \( \alpha \) is arbitrary, we conclude by \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} once again that
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( x \in \naturalset \) implies \( x \union \mleft\{ x \mright\} \in \naturalset \)}
    \end{equation*}
    Given that \( \emptyset \in \naturalset \), \( \naturalset \) is necessarily inductive by \cref{dfn:metatheory-inductive-set}.
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}[Weak Induction]
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-weak-induction}
    Let \( P(n) \) be a predicate where the following conditions hold:
    \begin{enumerate}[topsep=2pt,label=\textup{\texttt{(\alph*)}}]
        \item \( P(\emptyset) \) is a true statement
        \item for all \( n \), if \( P(n) \) is a true statement, then \( P(n \union \mleft\{ n \mright\}) \) is also a true statement
    \end{enumerate}
    Then \( \naturalset = \setbuilder{ n \in \naturalset }{P(n)} \).
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( S \) denote the set \( \setbuilder{n \in \naturalset}{P(n)} \) whose existence is guaranteed by \cref{meta-axiom:specification}.
    We begin by proving that \( S \) is an inductive set.
    Note that \( P(\emptyset) \) is necessarily a true statement by the definition of \( P(n) \) itself.
    Furthermore, we know from \cref{thm:metatheory-the-set-of-all-natural-numbers-is-inductive} that \( \naturalset \) is inductive,
    which means that \( \emptyset \in \naturalset \) by \cref{dfn:metatheory-inductive-set}.
    In other words, \( P(\emptyset) \) and \( \emptyset \in \naturalset \) are both true statements.
    Therefore, \( \emptyset \in S \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Now let \( \alpha \) denote an arbitrary object.
    Suppose that \( \alpha \in S \); that is, \( \alpha \in \naturalset \) and \( P(\alpha) \) are both true.
    But if \( P(\alpha) \) is true, \( P(\alpha \union \mleft\{ \alpha \mright\}) \) must also be a true statement
    by the definition of \( P(n) \).
    Furthermore, \( \alpha \union \mleft\{ \alpha \mright\} \in \naturalset \) is also true by \cref{dfn:metatheory-inductive-set}
    since \( \naturalset \) is inductive.
    In other words, \( \alpha \union \mleft\{ \alpha \mright\} \in \naturalset \) and \( P(\alpha \union \mleft\{ \alpha \mright\}) \) are both
    true statements.
    Therefore, \( \alpha \union \mleft\{ \alpha \mright\} \in S \).
    This means that \( \alpha \in S \) implies \( \alpha \union \mleft\{ \alpha \mright\} \).
    Since \( \alpha \) is arbitrary, we conclude by \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} that
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( x \), \( x \in S \) implies \( x \union \mleft\{ x \mright\} \in S \)}
    \end{equation*}
    Since \( \emptyset \in S \) is also true, it follows from \cref{dfn:metatheory-inductive-set} that \( S \) is an inductive set.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    We already know from \cref{thm:metatheory-restricted-comprehension-set-is-the-subset-of-an-existing-set} that \( S \subseteq \naturalset \).
    It remains to show that \( \naturalset \subseteq S \).
    Let \( \beta \) denote an arbitrary object.
    Suppose that \( \beta \in \naturalset \).
    Now consider the definition of \( \naturalset \) as stated in \cref{dfn:metatheory-the-set-of-all-natural-numbers}.
    Since \( \beta \in \naturalset \), it follows that
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( I \), if \( I \) is inductive, then \( \beta \in I \)}
    \end{equation*}
    By \cref{meta-rule:universal-instantiation}, we further infer that if \( S \) is inductive, then \( \beta \in S \).
    But we have already shown that \( S \) is inductive. Thus, by \cref{meta-rule:modus-ponens}, \( \beta \in S \) is necessarily true.
    In other words, \( \beta \in \naturalset \) implies \( \beta \in S \).
    Since \( \beta \) denotes an arbitrary object, \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization} is applicable and we have
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( x \), if \( x \in \naturalset \), then \( x \in S \)}
    \end{equation*}
    By \cref{dfn:metatheory-subsets-and-proper-subsets}, it follows that \( \naturalset \subseteq S \).
    Since \( \naturalset \) and \( S \) are subsets of each other, \cref{thm:metatheory-equality-in-terms-of-subsets} guarantees that
    \( \naturalset = S = \setbuilder{n \in \naturalset}{P(n)} \).
\end{proof}

\begin{rem}
    The Principle of Weak Induction as stated in \cref{thm:metatheory-weak-induction} essentially says that if we have a
    predicate about the natural numbers such that \( P(0) \) is true and that \( P(n) \) implies \( P(n + 1) \),
    then \( P(n) \) can be satisfied by all elements of \( \naturalset \).
\end{rem}

\begin{dfn}
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:metatheory-base-case-and-inductive-step}
    In any application of the \aref{thm:metatheory-weak-induction}{Principle of Weak Induction} on a predicate \( P(n) \),
    the statement \( P(\emptyset) \) is known as the \keyword{base case} whereas
    the statement ``for all \( n \), if \( n \in \naturalset \), then \( P(n) \) implies \( P(n + 1) \)''
    is known as the \keyword{inductive step}.
\end{dfn}

\begin{thm}[Strong Induction]
    \phantomsection \label{thm:metatheory-strong-induction}
    Let \( P(n) \) be any predicate such that
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( n \in \naturalset \), \( Q(n) \) implies \( P(n) \) }
    \end{equation*}
    where \( Q(n) \) is the statement ``for all \( m < n \), \( P(m) \)''.
    Then \( \naturalset = \setbuilder{n \in \naturalset}{P(n)} \).
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    By assumption, we know that
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:Q-n-initial-assumption}
        \text{for all \( n \in \naturalset \), \( Q(n) \) implies \( P(n) \) }
    \end{equation}
    The statement \( Q(\emptyset) \) can be translated as
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all \( m < \emptyset \), \( P(m) \)}
    \end{equation*}
    But \( \emptyset \) is empty, so this statement is \aref{dfn:metatheory-vacuous-truth}{vacuously true}.
    This means that \( Q(\emptyset) \) is true, thus establishing the base case.
    To establish the inductive step, let \( n^{\prime} \) denote an arbitrary element of \( \naturalset \).
    Suppose that \( Q(n^{\prime}) \) is a true statement; that is,
    \begin{equation}
        \phantomsection \label{eq:Q-n-prime-statement}
        \text{for all \( m < n^{\prime} \), \( P(m) \)}
    \end{equation}
    Let \( e \) denote an arbitrary element of \( n^{\prime} + 1 \).
    Since \( n^{\prime} + 1 = n^{\prime} \union \mleft\{ n^{\prime} \mright\} \), it follows that either \( e = n^{\prime} \) or \( e \in n^{\prime} \).
    If \( e = n^{\prime} \), then \( P(e) \) must be true because \( P(n^{\prime}) \) is true as a result of applying
    \cref{meta-rule:universal-instantiation} to \eqref{eq:Q-n-initial-assumption}.
    If \( e \in n^{\prime} \), then \( P(e) \) is also true by \eqref{eq:Q-n-prime-statement}.
    Since \( e \) is an arbitrary element of \( n^{\prime} + 1 \), we conclude from \cref{meta-rule:universal-generalization}
    that \( P(m) \) is true for all \( m < n^{\prime} + 1 \).
    In other words, \( Q(n^{\prime} + 1) \) must be true whenever \( Q(n^{\prime}) \) is true, which establishes the inductive step.
    Having established the base case and the inductive step, we can validly conclude by \aref{thm:metatheory-weak-induction}{Weak Induction} that
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:Q-n-true-for-all-natural-numbers}
        \text{for all \( n \in \naturalset \), \( Q(n) \)}
    \end{equation}
    Now let \( \alpha \) denote an arbitrary element of \( \naturalset \). From \eqref{eq:Q-n-initial-assumption} and \eqref{eq:Q-n-true-for-all-natural-numbers},
    it follows that \( P(\alpha) \) must be true.
    Since \( \alpha \) is arbitrary, we conclude that \( P(n) \) is true for all \( n \in \naturalset \). In other words,
    \( \naturalset \subseteq \setbuilder{n \in \naturalset}{P(n)} \).
    Furthermore, \( \setbuilder{n \in \naturalset}{P(n)} \subseteq \naturalset \) is also true
    by \cref{thm:metatheory-restricted-comprehension-set-is-the-subset-of-an-existing-set}.
    Since \( \naturalset \) and \( \setbuilder{n \in \naturalset}{P(n)} \) are subsets of each other, it follows from
    \cref{thm:metatheory-equality-in-terms-of-subsets} that \( \naturalset = \setbuilder{n \in \naturalset}{P(n)} \).
\end{proof}

\section{First-Order Languages}
Suppose we have at our disposal a non-empty, countable set \( \Sigma \) of symbolic entities---i.e., glyphs and characters that can be drawn, written, or printed
on a sheet of paper.
If we now take \( \mathcal{F} \) to be any non-empty subset of \( \finiteseq{\Sigma} \), then we say that \( \mathcal{F} \) is a \keyword{formal language} over \( \Sigma \)
and that \( \Sigma \) is the \keyword{alphabet} of \( \mathcal{F} \).
Although it is possible (at least in principle) to pick arbitrary elements of \( \finiteseq{\Sigma} \) to include in \( \mathcal{F} \), it is more common for
\( \mathcal{F} \) to be defined using a set of precise rules called the \keyword{syntax} of \( \mathcal{F} \).

\vspace{0.3cm}
\noindent
A great number of formal languages have been invented throughout history, each of which requires a separate book to expound.
Therefore, we will restrict ourselves to a class of formal languages that are particularly suited to express mathematical statements: \keyword{first-order languages}.

\begin{dfn}
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:first-order-language}
    The alphabet of a first-order language \( \mathcal{L} \) consists of:
    \begin{enumerate}[topsep=2pt,label=\textup{\texttt{(\alph*)}}]
        \item Logical Symbols:
            \begin{enumerate}[topsep=2pt,label=\arabic*.]
                \item Parentheses: \( () \, , \,[ \, ] \)
                \item Sentential Connectives\footnote{All the other familiar connectives can be defined in terms of \( \lnot \), \( \land \), and \( \lor \).}:
                    \( \lnot \), \( \land \), \( \lor \)
                \item Quantifiers: \( \forall \), \( \exists \)
                \item Equality(optional): \( = \)
                \item Variables: \( v_{0}, v_{1}, \dots \)
            \end{enumerate}
        \item Non-Logical Symbols:
            \begin{enumerate}[topsep=2pt,label=\arabic*.]
                \item Predicates: A tuple \( P^{\mathcal{L}} \) of predicate symbols with a function
                    \( \function{\Pi^{\mathcal{L}}}{\dom P^{\mathcal{L}}}{\naturalset^{+} } \) associated with it
                \item Functions: A tuple \( F^{\mathcal{L}} \) of function symbols with a function
                    \( \function{\Phi^{\mathcal{L}}}{\dom F^{\mathcal{L}}}{\naturalset} \) associated with it
                    \phantomsection \label{text:first-order-language-canonical-well-ordering-for-variable-symbols}
            \end{enumerate}
    \end{enumerate}
\end{dfn}

\begin{rem}
    The functions \( \Pi^{\mathcal{L}} \) and \( \Phi^{\mathcal{L}} \) are called the arity functions of \( P^{\mathcal{L}} \) and \( F^{\mathcal{L}} \) respectively.
    Also, the traditional ``constant'' symbols are treated as \( 0 \)-ary function symbols in this approach.
    That is why \( \ran \Phi^{\mathcal{L}} \) can be the entirety of \( \naturalset \) (which includes 0) whereas \( 0 \) must
    be excluded from the range of \( \Pi^{\mathcal{L}} \).
\end{rem}

\begin{dfn}
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:first-order-language-L-symbol}
    A symbol that belongs to the alphabet of \( \mathcal{L} \) is called an \( \mathcal{L} \)-symbol.
\end{dfn}

\begin{dfn}
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:first-order-language-L-variable}
    An \( \mathcal{L} \)-symbol that is also a variable symbol is called an \( \mathcal{L} \)-variable.
\end{dfn}

\begin{dfn}
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:first-order-language-L-string}
    An \( \mathcal{L} \)-string is a finite sequence of \( \mathcal{L} \)-symbols.
\end{dfn}

\begin{dfn}
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:first-order-language-L-expression}
    An \( \mathcal{L} \)-expression is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-string that belongs to \( \mathcal{L} \).
    If \( \varepsilon \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-expression, then \( \folsymbols{ \varepsilon } \) denotes the set of all
    the \( \mathcal{L} \)-symbols consituting \( \varepsilon \).
\end{dfn}

\begin{dfn}
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:first-order-language-the-set-of-all-variable-symbols-in-a-first-order-language}
    If \( \mathcal{L} \) is a first-order language, then \( \folvars{\mathcal{L}} \) denotes the (countable and non-empty) set of all \( \mathcal{L} \)-variables
    associated with \( \mathcal{L} \).
    If \( \varepsilon \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-expression, then \( \folvars{ \varepsilon } \) denotes the set of all \( \mathcal{L} \)-variables
    appearing in \( \varepsilon \).
\end{dfn}

\begin{cor}
    It follows then that for any \( \mathcal{L} \)-expression \( \varepsilon \), \( \folvars{ \varepsilon } \subseteq \folsymbols{ \varepsilon } \)
    and \( \folvars{ \varepsilon } \subseteq \folvars{\mathcal{L}} \).
\end{cor}

\subsection{Syntax}
The syntax of first-order languages is relatively straightforward.
In first-order languages, the most basic unit of grammar is formulas---analogous to
clauses in natural languages. But just as clauses are composed of words (verbs, nouns, etc.) in that natural language,
formulas are also composed of smaller units called \emph{terms}.

\begin{dfn}[Terms]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:first-order-language-construction-of-terms}
    An \aref{dfn:first-order-language-L-expression}{\( \mathcal{L} \)-expression} is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-term if and only if
    it is constructible in a finite number of steps via the application of the following two rules:
    \begin{enumerate}[topsep=2pt,label=\textup{\texttt{(\alph*)}}]
        \item If \( t \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-expression such that \( \folsymbols{t} = \mleft\{ x \mright\} \) and \( x \in \folvars{\mathcal{L}} \),
             then \( t \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-term
        \item If \( \tau \) is a tuple of \( \mathcal{L} \)-terms such that \( \dom \tau = \Phi^{\mathcal{L}}(i) \) for some \( i \in \dom F^{\mathcal{L}} \),
             then \( F^{\mathcal{L}}_{i}(\tau) \) is also an \( \mathcal{L} \)-term
    \end{enumerate}
\end{dfn}

\begin{dfn}[Complexity]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:first-order-language-complexity-of-terms}
    If \( t \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-term, then the \keyword{complexity} of \( t \) (denoted as \( \folcomplexityofterm{t} \))
    is the number of occurrences of function symbols with non-zero arity in \( t \).
\end{dfn}

\begin{rem}
    Note that the complexity of a term counts the number of \emph{occurrences} of function symbols with non-zero arity, not just the number of such function symbols occurring
    in \( t \). For instance, a term \( F(G(x), G(y)) \) has a complexity of \( 3 \) not \( 2 \) even though only two function symbols occurred in it in total.
    The function symbol \( G \) needs to be counted twice.
\end{rem}

\begin{cor}
    \phantomsection \label{cor:first-order-language-every-term-has-finite-complexity}
    If \( t \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-term, then \( \folcomplexityofterm{t} \in \naturalset \).
\end{cor}

\begin{cor}
    \phantomsection \label{cor:first-order-language-terms-with-zero-complexity-is-either-a-variable-or-0-ary-function-symbol}
    If \( t \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-term and \( \folcomplexityofterm{t} = 0 \), then either \( t \) comprises of a single \( \mathcal{L} \)-variable
    or \( t \) has the form \( F^{\mathcal{L}}_{i}() \) for some \( i \in \dom F^{\mathcal{L}} \) such that \( \Phi^{\mathcal{L}}(i) = 0 \).
\end{cor}

\begin{cor}
    \phantomsection \label{cor:first-order-language-terms-with-non-zero-complexity-must-have-a-parent-function-symbol}
    If \( t \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-term and \( \folcomplexityofterm{t} \neq 0 \), then \( t \) must have the form
    \( F^{\mathcal{L}}_{i}(\tau) \) for some \( i \in \dom F^{\mathcal{L}} \) where \( \tau \) is a tuple of \( \mathcal{L} \)-terms
    and \( \abs{\tau} = \Phi^{\mathcal{L}}(i) \neq 0 \).
\end{cor}

\begin{eg}
    Thus, in a first-order language \( \mathcal{L} \) with function symbols \( F \), \( G \), and \( H \) (with arities 0, 1, and 2 respectively) and variable symbols
    \( x \), \( y \), and \( z \), we have the following:
    \( \folcomplexityofterm{x} = 0 \),
    \( \folcomplexityofterm{F()} = 0 \),
    \( \folcomplexityofterm{G(x)} = 1 \),
    \( \folcomplexityofterm{H(F(), y)} = 1 \),
    \( \folcomplexityofterm{H(G(x), z)} = 2 \),
    \( \folcomplexityofterm{H(H(x, F()), y)} = 2 \).
\end{eg}

\begin{dfn}[Subterms]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:first-order-language-subterms}
    If \( s \) and \( t \) denote \( \mathcal{L} \)-terms,
    then we say that \( s \) is a \keyword{subterm} of \( t \) (denoted as \( s \folsubterm t \))
    if and only if the following conditions are met:
    \begin{enumerate}[topsep=2pt,label=\textup{\texttt{(\alph*)}}]
        \item \( t \) has the form \( F^{\mathcal{L}}_{i}(\tau) \) for some \( i \in \dom F^{\mathcal{L}} \) where \( \tau \) is a tuple of \( \mathcal{L} \)-terms
            such that \( \abs{\tau} = \Phi^{\mathcal{L}}(i) \neq 0 \)
        \item \( s \in \ran \tau \) or \( s \folsubterm r \) for some \( r \in \ran \tau \)
    \end{enumerate}
\end{dfn}

\begin{cor}
    \phantomsection \label{cor:first-order-language-the-complexity-of-a-subterm-is-less-than-the-complexity-of-the-parent-term}
    If \( t \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-term and \( s \) is a subterm of \( t \), then \( \folcomplexityofterm{s} < \folcomplexityofterm{t} \).
\end{cor}

\begin{dfn}[Atoms]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:first-order-language-atom}
    An \( \mathcal{L} \)-expression \( \varphi \) is an \keyword{atom} of \( \mathcal{L} \) if and only if
    \( \varphi \) has either one of the following forms:
    \begin{enumerate}[topsep=2pt,label=\textup{\texttt{(\alph*)}}]
        \item \( s = t \) where \( s \) and \( t \) are \aref{dfn:first-order-language-construction-of-terms}{\( \mathcal{L} \)-terms}
        \item \( P^{\mathcal{L}}_{i}(\tau) \) for some \( i \in \dom P^{\mathcal{L}} \) where
            \( \tau \) is a tuple of \( \mathcal{L} \)-terms such that \( \abs{\tau} = \Pi^{\mathcal{L}}(i) \)
    \end{enumerate}
\end{dfn}

\begin{dfn}[Formulas]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:first-order-language-construction-of-formulas}
    An \( \mathcal{L} \)-expression is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula\footnote{\,Also called a \keyword{well-formed formula} or wff in \( \mathcal{L} \).}
    if and only if it is constructible from atoms of \( \mathcal{L} \) in a finite number of steps via the application of the following rules:
    \begin{enumerate}[topsep=2pt,label=\textup{\texttt{(\alph*)}}]
        \item If \( \varphi \) is an atom of \( \mathcal{L} \), then \( \varphi \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula
        \item If \( \varphi \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula, then \( \mleft( \varphi \mright) \) is also an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula
        \item If \( \varphi \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula, then \( \mleft[ \, \varphi \, \mright] \) is also an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula
        \item If \( \varphi \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula, then \( \lnot \, \varphi \) is also an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula
        \item If \( \varphi \) and \( \psi \) are \( \mathcal{L} \)-formulas, then \( \varphi \,\,{\land}\,\, \psi \) is also an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula
        \item If \( \varphi \) and \( \psi \) are \( \mathcal{L} \)-formulas, then \( \varphi \,\,{\lor}\,\, \psi \) is also an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula
        \item If \( \varphi \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula and \( v \) is any \( \mathcal{L} \)-variable,
            then \( \forall v \, \varphi \) is also an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula
        \item If \( \varphi \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula and \( v \) is any \( \mathcal{L} \)-variable,
            then \( \exists v \, \varphi \) is also an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula
    \end{enumerate}
\end{dfn}

\begin{notation}
    The familiar abbreviations from propositional logic do apply here, of course. For instance, if \( \varphi \) and \( \psi \) are
    \( \mathcal{L} \)-formulas, then we write \( \varphi {\implies} \psi \) to abbreviate \( \lnot \, \varphi \,\,{\lor}\,\, \psi \),
    \( \varphi {\iff} \psi \) to abbreviate \( \mleft( \varphi {\implies} \psi \mright) \,\,{\land}\,\, \mleft( \psi {\implies} \varphi \mright)  \), and so on.
\end{notation}

\begin{dfn}[Syntactic Operations]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:first-order-language-syntactic-operations}
    If \( \mathcal{L} \) is a first-order language, then \( \mathcal{R}_{\ast} \) denotes an operation that can be applied to formulas of \( \mathcal{L} \)
    to construct a new formulas of \( \mathcal{L} \).
    The operation \( \mathcal{R}_{\ast} \) is also called a \keyword{syntactic operation} of \( \mathcal{L} \).
    There are exactly 7 syntactic operations in our approach:  \( \mathcal{R}_{(} \), \( \mathcal{R}_{[} \),
    \( \mathcal{R}_{\lnot} \), \( \mathcal{R}_{\land} \), \( \mathcal{R}_{\lor} \), \( \mathcal{R}_{\forall} \), and \( \mathcal{R}_{\exists} \).
\end{dfn}

\begin{eg}
    If \( \varphi \) and \( \psi \) are \( \mathcal{L} \)-formulas, then \( \varphi \,\,{\land}\,\, \psi \) is the formula obtained by applying
    the syntactic operation \( \mathcal{R}_{\land} \) to \( \varphi \) and \( \psi \), \( \varphi \,\,{\lor}\,\, \psi \) is the formula obtained
    by applying the syntactic operation \( \mathcal{R}_{\lor} \) to \( \varphi \) and \( \psi \), etc.
\end{eg}

\begin{dfn}[Complexity]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:first-order-language-complexity-of-formulas}
    If \( \varphi \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula, then the \keyword{complexity} of \( \varphi \) (denoted as \( \folcomplexityofformula{\varphi} \))
    is the total number of syntactic operations required to construct \( \varphi \) from atoms of \( \mathcal{L} \).
    More precisely:
    \begin{enumerate}[topsep=2pt,label=\textup{\texttt{(\alph*)}}]
        \item If \( \varphi \coloneq \mleft( \psi \mright) \), \( \varphi \coloneq \mleft[ \,\psi\, \mright] \), \( \varphi \coloneq \lnot \, \psi \),
            \( \varphi \coloneq \forall x \, \psi \), or \( \varphi \coloneq \exists x \, \psi \),
            then \( \folcomplexityofformula{\varphi} = \folcomplexityofformula{\psi} + 1 \)
        \item If \( \varphi \coloneq \psi \,\,{\land}\,\, \phi \) or \( \varphi \coloneq \psi \,\,{\lor}\,\, \phi \),
            then \( \folcomplexityofformula{\varphi} = \folcomplexityofformula{\psi} + \folcomplexityofformula{\phi} + 1 \)
    \end{enumerate}
\end{dfn}

\begin{cor}
    \phantomsection \label{cor:first-order-language-every-formula-has-finite-complexity}
    It follows immediately from \cref{dfn:first-order-language-construction-of-formulas} that every \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula can be constructed
    from \aref{dfn:first-order-language-atom}{atoms of \( \mathcal{L} \)} via the application of a finite number of
    \aref{dfn:first-order-language-syntactic-operations}{syntactic operations}; that is, for every \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula \( \varphi \),
    \( \folcomplexityofformula{\varphi} \in \naturalset \).
\end{cor}

\begin{cor}
    \phantomsection \label{cor:first-order-language-only-atoms-can-have-zero-complexity}
    If \( \varphi \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula, then \( \folcomplexityofformula{\varphi} = 0 \) iff \( \varphi \) is an atom.
\end{cor}

\begin{cor}
    \phantomsection \label{cor:first-order-language-every-formula-with-non-zero-complexity-can-be-decomposed}
    If \( \varphi \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula such that \( \folcomplexityofformula{\varphi} \neq 0 \), then exactly one of the following must be true:
    \begin{enumerate}[topsep=2pt,label=\textup{\texttt{(\alph*)}}]
        \item \( \varphi \coloneq \mleft( \psi \mright) \) where \( \psi \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula
        \item \( \varphi \coloneq \mleft[ \, \psi \, \mright] \) where \( \psi \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula
        \item \( \varphi \coloneq \lnot \, \psi \) where \( \psi \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula
        \item \( \varphi \coloneq \psi \,\,{\land}\,\, \phi \) or \( \varphi \coloneq \phi \,\,{\land}\,\, \psi \)
            where \( \psi \) and \( \phi \) are \( \mathcal{L} \)-formulas
        \item \( \varphi \coloneq \psi \,\,{\lor}\,\, \phi \) or \( \varphi \coloneq \phi \,\,{\lor}\,\, \psi \) where \( \psi \) and \( \phi \)
            are \( \mathcal{L} \)-formulas
        \item \( \varphi \coloneq \forall x \, \psi \) where \( \psi \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula and \( x \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-variable
        \item \( \varphi \coloneq \exists x \, \psi \) where \( \psi \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula and \( x \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-variable
    \end{enumerate}
\end{cor}

\begin{eg}
    If \( \varphi \) and \( \psi \) are atoms of \( \mathcal{L} \) and \( x \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-variable, then we have the following:
    \( \folcomplexityofformula{\varphi} = 0 \), \( \folcomplexityofformula{\forall x \, \varphi} = 1\), \(  \folcomplexityofformula{\varphi \,\,{\land}\,\, \psi} = 1 \),
    \( \folcomplexityofformula{\lnot \, \varphi \,\,{\land}\,\, \psi} = 2 \),
    \(  \folcomplexityofformula{\varphi \,\,{\lor}\,\, \psi \,\,{\land}\,\, \psi} = 2 \),
    \( \folcomplexityofformula{\mleft( \varphi \,\,{\lor}\,\, \psi \mright) \,\,{\land}\,\, \psi} = 3 \), etc.
\end{eg}

\begin{dfn}[Subformulas]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:first-order-language-subformulas}
    If \( \varphi \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula, then any contiguous subsequence of \( \varphi \) that is also an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula
    is called a \keyword{subformula} of \( \varphi \). More precisely, if \( \varphi \) and \( \psi \) are \( \mathcal{L} \)-formulas, then
    \( \psi \) is a subformula of \( \varphi \) (denoted as \( \psi \folsubformula \varphi \)) if and only if exactly one of the following conditions is met:
    \begin{enumerate}[topsep=2pt,label=\textup{\texttt{(\alph*)}}]
        \item \( \varphi \coloneq \mleft( \psi \mright) \)
        \item \( \varphi \coloneq \mleft[ \, \psi \, \mright] \)
        \item \( \varphi \coloneq \lnot \, \psi \)
        \item \( \varphi \coloneq \phi \,\,{\land}\,\, \psi \) or \( \varphi \coloneq \psi \,\,{\land}\,\, \phi \) for some \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula \( \phi \)
        \item \( \varphi \coloneq \phi \,\,{\lor}\,\, \psi \) or \( \varphi \coloneq \psi \,\,{\lor}\,\, \phi\) for some \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula \( \phi \)
        \item \( \varphi \coloneq \forall x \, \psi \) for some \( \mathcal{L} \)-variable \( x \)
        \item \( \varphi \coloneq \exists x \, \psi \) for some \( \mathcal{L} \)-variable \( x \)
        \item \( \psi \folsubformula \phi \) for some \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula \( \phi \) such that \( \phi \folsubformula \varphi \)
    \end{enumerate}
\end{dfn}

\begin{cor}
    \phantomsection \label{cor:first-order-language-the-complexity-of-a-subformula-is-less-than-the-complexity-of-the-formula}
    If \( \psi \folsubformula \varphi \), then \( \folcomplexityofformula{\psi} < \folcomplexityofformula{\varphi} \).
\end{cor}

\begin{rem}
    The converse of \cref{cor:first-order-language-the-complexity-of-a-subformula-is-less-than-the-complexity-of-the-formula} is obviously false.
    Here is a simple counter-example with \( \varphi \coloneq \mleft( x = y \mright) \,\,{\land}\,\, \mleft( z \neq x \mright)  \) and \( \psi \coloneq \mleft( x \neq y \mright) \).
    Clearly, \( \folcomplexityofformula{\psi} = 1 < 4 = \folcomplexityofformula{\varphi} \), but \( \psi \) is \emph{not} a subformula of \( \varphi \).
\end{rem}

\begin{notation}
    If \( \varphi \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula and \( \psi \folsubformula \varphi \), then \( \folsubformulasubstitution{\varphi}{\psi}{\phi} \)
    denotes the \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula obtained after every occurrence of \( \psi \) in \( \varphi \)
    has been replaced with \( \phi \).
\end{notation}

\begin{eg}
    If \( \varphi \coloneq \mleft( x = y \mright) \,\,{\land}\,\, \mleft( z \neq x \mright) \,\,{\lor}\,\, \mleft( x = y \mright) \),
    \( \psi \coloneq \mleft( x = y \mright) \), and
    \( \phi \coloneq x \neq y \), then \( \folsubformulasubstitution{\varphi}{\psi}{\phi} = \mleft( x \neq y \mright) \,\,{\land}\,\, \mleft( z \neq x \mright) \,\,{\lor}\,\, \mleft( x \neq y \mright) \).
\end{eg}

\begin{dfn}
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:first-order-language-variable-occurrence-in-formulas}
    If \( \varphi \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula and \( v \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-variable, then we say that ``\( v \) occurs in \( \varphi \),'' 
    ``\( v \) is in \( \varphi \),'' or ``\( \varphi \) contains \( v \)'' if and only if \( v \in \folvars{\varphi} \).
\end{dfn}

\begin{dfn}[Free Variables]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:first-order-language-recursive-definition-of-free-variables}
    If \( \varphi \) and \( \psi \) are \( \mathcal{L} \)-formulas, \( x \) and \( v \) are \( \mathcal{L} \)-variables, and \( \tau \) is a tuple of \( \mathcal{L} \)-variables,
    then the following holds:
    \begin{enumerate}[topsep=2pt,label=\textup{\texttt{(\alph*)}}]
        \item If \( \varphi \) is an atom of \( \mathcal{L} \), then \( x \) is a free variable of \( \varphi \) if and only if \( x \in \folvars{\varphi} \)
        \item \( x \) is a free variable of \( \lnot \, \varphi \) if and only if \( x \) is a free variable of \( \varphi \)
        \item \( x \) is a free variable of \( \varphi \,\,{\land}\,\, \psi \) if and only if \( x \) is a free variable of \( \varphi \) or \( \psi \)
        \item \( x \) is a free variable of \( \varphi \,\,{\lor}\,\, \psi \) if and only if \( x \) is a free variable of \( \varphi \) or \( \psi \)
        \item \( x \) is a free variable of \( \forall v \, \varphi \) if and only if \( x \) is a free variable of \( \varphi \) and \( x \neq v \)
        \item \( x \) is a free variable of \( \exists v \, \varphi \) if and only if \( x \) is a free variable of \( \varphi \) and \( x \neq v \)
    \end{enumerate}
\end{dfn}

\begin{rem}
    If \( x \) is a free variable of \( \varphi \), we also say that \( x \) \keyword{occurs freely} or \keyword{occurs free} in \( \varphi \).
\end{rem}

\begin{dfn}[Bound Variables]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:first-order-language-bound-variables}
    An \( \mathcal{L} \)-variable occurring in \( \varphi \) that is not free is called a \keyword{bound variable} of \( \varphi \). 
\end{dfn}

\begin{rem}
    Note that it is possible for the same variable symbol to be both free and bound in the same formula.
    For example, the variable symbol \( x \) in the formula \( \forall x \, \psi \,\,{\land}\,\, \mleft( x = y \mright) \)
    is both free and bound.
\end{rem}

\begin{dfn}
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:first-order-language-the-set-of-all-free-variables-bound-variables-of-a-formula}
    If \( \varphi \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula, then \( \folfreevars{\varphi} \) denotes the set of all free variables of \( \varphi \) and
    \( \folboundvars{\varphi} \) denotes the set of all bound variables of \( \varphi \).
\end{dfn}

\begin{cor}
    \phantomsection \label{cor:first-order-language-set-of-all-free-variables-is-a-subset-of-set-of-all-variables-the-definition-of-the-set-of-all-bound-variables}
    Naturally, \( \folfreevars{\varphi} \subseteq \folvars{\varphi} \) and \( \folboundvars{\varphi} = \folvars{\varphi} - \folfreevars{\varphi} \)
    for any \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula \( \varphi \).
\end{cor}

\begin{dfn}
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:first-order-language-free-variable-replacement-in-formulas}
    If \( \varphi \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula and \( x \), \( y \) are \( \mathcal{L} \)-variables,
    then \( \folfreevariablereplacement{\varphi}{x}{y} \) denotes the formula obtained after all free occurrences of \( x \) in \( \varphi \) has been
    replaced with \( y \).
\end{dfn}

\begin{notation}
    If \( \varphi \) has only one free variable \( x \), then we can abbreviate \( \folfreevariablereplacement{\varphi}{x}{y} \) with \( \varphi(y) \).
\end{notation}

\subsection{Semantics}
\noindent
The syntactic rules of \( \mathcal{L} \) are fully defined in the previous section;
that is, given any finite string of symbols in \aref{dfn:first-order-language}{\( \mathcal{L} \)}, we
now have the information at our disposal to determine whether that string of symbols is a valid formula of \( \mathcal{L} \).
Nevertheless, our description of \( \mathcal{L} \) is far from complete. For any language to be useful, having a bunch of symbols
and a set of rules to construct valid formulas using those symbols is not enough; there also needs to be a systematic way for
\emph{meaning} to be assigned to all valid formulas---just as English words like ``cats'', ``apples'', and ``oranges'' need to have meaning
for English to have any value at all as a language.

\vspace{0.3cm}
\noindent
The assignment of meaning to the expressions of a formal language is called the \keyword{semantics} of a formal language. This typically involves
associating the symbols in the language (and the valid expressions constructed using those symbols) with existing concepts or mental imageries.
These mental imageries can be either concrete or abstract. For instance, when a native English speaker sees the word ``apple,'' their minds
construct the image of a fruit with red skin. In other words, the semantics of a language allows the language to be \keyword{interpreted};
it provides a connection between a string of seemingly arbitrary symbols and concepts from the real world.

\vspace{0.3cm}
\noindent
In first-order languages, meaning is assigned to formulas via a special class of \aref{dfn:general-mathematical-structures}{mathematical structures}
known as \keyword{first-order structures}.

\begin{dfn}
    A \keyword{first-order structure} is any \aref{dfn:general-mathematical-structures}{mathematical structure} \( \mathfrak{A} \) such that
    \aref{notation:individual-components-of-mathematical-structures}{\( S^{\mathfrak{A}} = \emptyset \)}
    and \aref{notation:individual-components-of-mathematical-structures}{\( G^{\mathfrak{A}} = \emptyset \)}.
    In other words, a first-order structure is any mathematical structure that is endowed with no other features
    aside from n-ary \aref{dfn:n-ary-relation-on-a-set}{relations} and \aref{dfn:n-ary-operation}{operations}.
\end{dfn}

\noindent
Of course, not every first-order structure can be used to assign meaning to \( \mathcal{L} \)-formulas.
In fact, for a first-order structure to be capable of interpreting formulas of \( \mathcal{L} \),
its \aref{dfn:signature-of-a-structure}{signature} must be compatible with the arity functions
\aref{dfn:first-order-language}{\( \Pi^{\mathcal{L}} \) and \( \Phi^{\mathcal{L}} \)},
which leads us to the following definition.

\begin{dfn}
    An \( \mathcal{L} \)-structure is any first-order structure with \aref{dfn:signature-of-a-structure}{signature}
    \( \tuple{\tuple{}, \Pi^{\mathcal{L}}, \Phi^{\mathcal{L}}, \tuple{}} \).
\end{dfn}

\begin{rem}
    \phantomsection \label{one-to-one-correspondences-between-first-order-language-and-first-order-structure}
    Note that \( F^{\mathcal{L}} \), \( P^{\mathcal{L}} \), \( F^{\mathfrak{A}} \), and \( R^{\mathfrak{A}} \) are all bijective tuples
    because the symbols associated with \( \mathcal{L} \) and \( \mathfrak{A} \) are bound to be unique.
    As a result, \( \composition{F^{\mathfrak{A}}}{\mleft( F^{\mathcal{L}} \mright)^{-1}} \) and
    \( \composition{R^{\mathfrak{A}}}{\mleft( P^{\mathcal{L}} \mright)^{-1}} \) are both bijections.
    In other words, there exist one-to-one correspondences between the function symbols of \( \mathcal{L} \) and the n-ary operations of \( \mathfrak{A} \)
    as well as between the predicate symbols of \( \mathcal{L} \) and the n-ary relation of \( \mathfrak{A} \).
\end{rem}

\begin{dfn}
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:first-order-language-variable-assignment-function}
    If \( \mathfrak{A} \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-structure, then a function \( \mathfrak{a} \) is a \keyword{variable assignment function}
    of \( \mathcal{L} \) under \( \mathfrak{A} \) if and only if \( \function{\mathfrak{a}}{\folvars{\mathcal{L}}}{\abs{\mathfrak{A}}} \).
    In other words, any function on \( \folvars{\mathcal{L}} \) into \( \abs{\mathfrak{A}} \) is a variable assignment function of \( \mathcal{L} \) under \( \mathfrak{A} \).
\end{dfn}

\begin{dfn}
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:first-order-language-modified-variable-assignment-function}
    Let \( \mathfrak{A} \) be an \( \mathcal{L} \)-structure and \( \function{\mathfrak{a}}{\folvars{\mathcal{L}}}{\abs{\mathfrak{A}}} \) be a variable assignment function of
    \( \mathcal{L} \) under \( \mathfrak{A} \).
    If \( v \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-variable and \( e \in \abs{\mathfrak{A}} \), then \( \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{\mathfrak{a}}{v}{e} \) denotes
    the function \( \restrictedfunction{\mathfrak{a}}{ \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ v \mright\} } \union \big\{ \orderedpair{v}{e} \big\} \).
\end{dfn}

\begin{rem}
    Note that \( \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{\mathfrak{a}}{v}{e} \) is still a variable assignment function because
    \( \dom \big( \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{\mathfrak{a}}{v}{e} \big) = \folvars{\mathcal{L}} \)
    and \( \ran \big( \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{\mathfrak{a}}{v}{e} \big) \subseteq \abs{\mathfrak{A}} \).
\end{rem}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:first-order-language-existence-of-variable-assignment-function}
    For any first-order language \( \mathcal{L} \) and any \( \mathcal{L} \)-structure \( \mathfrak{A} \),
    a variable assignment function of \( \mathcal{L} \) under \( \mathfrak{A} \) exists.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( \mathfrak{A} \) be an arbitrary \( \mathcal{L} \)-structure.
    Since \( \folvars{\mathcal{L}} \) and \( \abs{\mathfrak{A}} \) are non-empty sets,
    it is easy to define a function on \( \folvars{\mathcal{L}} \) into \( \abs{\mathfrak{A}} \).
    E.g., given an arbitrary element \( \alpha \) of \( \abs{\mathfrak{A}} \),
    let \( \mathfrak{a} \) be a function such that \( \dom \mathfrak{a} = \folvars{\mathcal{L}} \) and \( \mathfrak{a}(x) = \alpha \) for all \( x \in \folvars{\mathcal{L}} \).
    By \cref{dfn:first-order-language-variable-assignment-function}, \( \mathfrak{a} \) is a variable assignment function of \( \mathcal{L} \) under \( \mathfrak{A} \).
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:first-order-language-the-last-modification-will-take-effect-if-the-same-variable-symbol-is-modified-for-a-variable-assignment-function}
    If \( \mathfrak{A} \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-structure, \( \mathfrak{a} \) is any variable assignment function of \( \mathcal{L} \) under \( \mathfrak{A} \),
    and \( x \) is any \( \mathcal{L} \)-variable, then
    \begin{equation*}
        \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{ \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{\mathfrak{a}}{x}{e_{0}} }{x}{e_{1}}
        = \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{\mathfrak{a}}{x}{e_{1}}
    \end{equation*}
    for all \( e_{0}, e_{1} \in \abs{\mathfrak{A}} \).
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( e_{0} \) and \( e_{1} \) denote arbitrary elements of \( \abs{\mathfrak{A}} \).
    According to \cref{dfn:first-order-language-variable-assignment-function}, we have
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:definition-of-a-modified-x-mapsto-e0}
        \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{ \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{\mathfrak{a}}{x}{e_{0}} }{x}{e_{1}}
        = \restrictedfunctionsmall{ \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{\mathfrak{a}}{x}{e_{0}} }{ \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x \mright\} } \union
        \mleft\{ \orderedpair{x}{e_{1}} \mright\}
    \end{equation}
    But \( \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{\mathfrak{a}}{x}{e_{0}} = \restrictedfunctionsmall{\mathfrak{a}}{ \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x \mright\} } \,{\union}\, \mleft\{ \orderedpair{x}{e_{0}} \mright\} \) so that
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:definition-of-a-modified-x-e0-restricted-to-var-L-minus-x}
        \restrictedfunctionsmall{ \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{\mathfrak{a}}{x}{e_{0}} }{ \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x \mright\} } =
        \restrictedfunctionsmall{ \mleft(  \restrictedfunctionsmall{\mathfrak{a}}{ \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x \mright\} } \,{\union}\, \mleft\{ \orderedpair{x}{e_{0}} \mright\} \mright) }{ \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x \mright\} }
    \end{equation}
    Since \(  \restrictedfunctionsmall{\mathfrak{a}}{ \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x \mright\} }  \) and \( \mleft\{ \orderedpair{x}{e_{0}} \mright\} \) are
    \aref{thm:the-union-of-a-compatible-system-of-functions-is-a-function}{compatible functions}, \cref{thm:metatheory-restriction-of-union-of-compatible-functions-equals-the-union-of-restrictions}
    applies and \eqref{eq:definition-of-a-modified-x-e0-restricted-to-var-L-minus-x} becomes
    \begin{align*}
        \label{eq:definition-of-a-modified-x-e0-restricted-to-var-L-minus-x-2}
        \restrictedfunctionsmall{ \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{\mathfrak{a}}{x}{e_{0}} }{ \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x \mright\} } &=
        \restrictedfunctionsmall{ \mleft(\restrictedfunctionsmall{\mathfrak{a}}{ \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x \mright\} }\mright) }{ \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x \mright\} }
        \,{\union}\,
        \restrictedfunctionsmall{ \mleft\{ \orderedpair{x}{e_{0}} \mright\} }{ \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x \mright\} }\\
        &= \restrictedfunctionsmall{ \mleft(\restrictedfunctionsmall{\mathfrak{a}}{ \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x \mright\} }\mright) }{ \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x \mright\} } \,{\union}\, \emptyset \\
        &= \restrictedfunctionsmall{ \mleft(\restrictedfunctionsmall{\mathfrak{a}}{ \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x \mright\} }\mright) }{ \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x \mright\} }
    \end{align*}
    But \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{ \mleft(\restrictedfunctionsmall{\mathfrak{a}}{ \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x \mright\} }\mright) }{ \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x \mright\} } = \restrictedfunctionsmall{\mathfrak{a}}{ \mleft( \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x \mright\} \mright) \,{\intersect}\, \mleft( \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x \mright\} \mright) } = \restrictedfunctionsmall{\mathfrak{a}}{ \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x \mright\} } \) by
    \cref{thm:metatheory-restriction-of-restricted-function-equals-original-function-restricted-to-intersection}.
    Thus, we have
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:a-modified-x-e0-restricted-to-var-L-minus-x-simplified-form}
        \restrictedfunctionsmall{ \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{\mathfrak{a}}{x}{e_{0}} }{ \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x \mright\} } =
        \restrictedfunctionsmall{\mathfrak{a}}{ \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x \mright\} }
    \end{equation}
    Substituting \eqref{eq:a-modified-x-e0-restricted-to-var-L-minus-x-simplified-form} back into \eqref{eq:definition-of-a-modified-x-mapsto-e0} yields
    \( \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{ \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{\mathfrak{a}}{x}{e_{0}} }{x}{e_{1}} =
    \restrictedfunctionsmall{\mathfrak{a}}{\folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x \mright\}} \,{\union}\, \mleft\{ \orderedpair{x}{e_{1}} \mright\} \).
    It should be obvious from \cref{dfn:first-order-language-modified-variable-assignment-function} that
    \( \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{ \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{\mathfrak{a}}{x}{e_{0}} }{x}{e_{1}} = \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{\mathfrak{a}}{x}{e_{1}} \).
\end{proof}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:first-order-language-the-order-of-variable-modification-of-modified-variable-assignment-functions-does-not-matter-as-long-as-the-variable-symbols-are-distinct}
    If \( \mathfrak{A} \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-structure, \( \mathfrak{a} \) is any variable assignment function of \( \mathcal{L} \) under \( \mathfrak{A} \), and
    \( x \) and \( y \) are two \emph{distinct} \( \mathcal{L} \)-variables (that is, \( x \neq y \)), then
    \begin{equation*}
        \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{ \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{\mathfrak{a}}{x}{e_{0}} }{y}{e_{1}} =
        \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{ \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{\mathfrak{a}}{y}{e_{1}} }{x}{e_{0}}
    \end{equation*}
    for all \( e_{0}, e_{1} \in \abs{\mathfrak{A}} \).
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Let \( e_{0} \) and \( e_{1} \) be arbitrary elements of \( \abs{\mathfrak{A}} \).
    We begin by writing out the full definition of \( \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{ \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{\mathfrak{a}}{x}{e_{0}} }{y}{e_{1}} \)
    according to \cref{dfn:first-order-language-modified-variable-assignment-function}.
    \begin{equation}
        \phantomsection \label{eq:definition-of-a-modified-x-e0-y-e1}
        \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{ \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{\mathfrak{a}}{x}{e_{0}} }{y}{e_{1}} =
        \restrictedfunctionsmall{ \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{\mathfrak{a}}{x}{e_{0}} }{ \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ y \mright\} }
        \union \{ \orderedpair{y}{e_{1}} \}
    \end{equation}
    But \( \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{\mathfrak{a}}{x}{e_{0}} = \restrictedfunctionsmall{\mathfrak{a}}{\folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x \mright\}} \union \mleft\{ \orderedpair{x}{e_{0}} \mright\} \) so that
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:definition-of-a-modified-x-e0-restricted-to-var-L-minus-y}
        \restrictedfunctionsmall{ \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{\mathfrak{a}}{x}{e_{0}} }{\folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ y \mright\}} =
        \restrictedfunctionsmall{ \mleft( \restrictedfunctionsmall{\mathfrak{a}}{ \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x \mright\} } \union \mleft\{ \orderedpair{x}{e_{0}} \mright\} \mright) }{\folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ y \mright\}}
    \end{equation}
    Since \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{\mathfrak{a}}{\folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x \mright\}} \) and \( \mleft\{ \orderedpair{x}{e_{0}} \mright\} \) are \aref{dfn:compatibility-of-a-system-of-functions}{compatible functions}, \cref{thm:metatheory-restriction-of-union-of-compatible-functions-equals-the-union-of-restrictions} applies and \eqref{eq:definition-of-a-modified-x-e0-restricted-to-var-L-minus-y} becomes
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:definition-of-a-modified-x-e0-restricted-to-var-L-minus-y-2}
        \restrictedfunctionsmall{ \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{\mathfrak{a}}{x}{e_{0}} }{\folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ y \mright\}} =
        \restrictedfunctionsmall{ \mleft(\restrictedfunctionsmall{\mathfrak{a}}{\folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x \mright\}}\mright) }{ \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ y \mright\} }
        \union \restrictedfunctionsmall{ \mleft\{ \orderedpair{x}{e_{0}} \mright\} }{ \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ y \mright\} }
    \end{equation}
    Note that \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{ \mleft\{ \orderedpair{x}{e_{0}} \mright\} }{\folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ y \mright\}} = \mleft\{ \orderedpair{x}{e_{0}} \mright\} \) because \( x \in \folvars{\mathcal{L}} \) and \( x \neq y \).
    Moreover,
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:restricted-restricted-a}
        \restrictedfunctionsmall{ \mleft( \restrictedfunctionsmall{\mathfrak{a}}{ \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x \mright\} } \mright) }{ \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ y \mright\} } = \restrictedfunctionsmall{\mathfrak{a}}{ \mleft( \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x \mright\} \mright) \,\,{\intersect}\,\,  \mleft( \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ y \mright\} \mright) }
    \end{equation}
    by \cref{thm:metatheory-restriction-of-restricted-function-equals-original-function-restricted-to-intersection}.
    Since \( \mleft( \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x \mright\} \mright) \,\,{\intersect}\,\, \mleft( \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ y \mright\} \mright) = \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft( \mleft\{ x \mright\} \union \mleft\{ y \mright\} \mright) \) and \( \mleft\{ x \mright\} \union \mleft\{ y \mright\} = \mleft\{ x, y \mright\} \) because
    \( x \) and \( y \) are distinct, \eqref{eq:restricted-restricted-a} becomes
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:restricted-restricted-a-2}
        \restrictedfunctionsmall{ \mleft( \restrictedfunctionsmall{\mathfrak{a}}{ \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x \mright\} } \mright) }{ \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ y \mright\} } = \restrictedfunctionsmall{\mathfrak{a}}{ \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x, y \mright\} }
    \end{equation}
    Substituting \eqref{eq:restricted-restricted-a-2} into \eqref{eq:definition-of-a-modified-x-e0-restricted-to-var-L-minus-y-2} yields
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:definition-of-a-modified-x-e0-restricted-to-var-L-minus-y-3}
        \restrictedfunctionsmall{ \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{\mathfrak{a}}{x}{e_{0}} }{\folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ y \mright\}} =
        \restrictedfunctionsmall{\mathfrak{a}}{ \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x, y \mright\} } \union \mleft\{ \orderedpair{x}{e_{0}} \mright\}
    \end{equation}
    Combining \eqref{eq:definition-of-a-modified-x-e0-restricted-to-var-L-minus-y-3} with \eqref{eq:definition-of-a-modified-x-e0-y-e1}, we have
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:definition-of-a-modified-x-e0-y-e1-fully-expanded-form}
        \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{ \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{\mathfrak{a}}{x}{e_{0}} }{y}{e_{1}} =
        \restrictedfunctionsmall{\mathfrak{a}}{ \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x, y \mright\} } \union \mleft\{ \orderedpair{x}{e_{0}} \mright\}
        \union \mleft\{ \orderedpair{y}{e_{1}} \mright\}
    \end{equation}
    We can apply the same process to \( \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{ \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{\mathfrak{a}}{y}{e_{1}} }{x}{e_{0}} \)
    and show that
    \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:definition-of-a-modified-y-e1-x-e0-fully-expanded-form}
        \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{ \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{\mathfrak{a}}{y}{e_{1}} }{x}{e_{0}} =
        \restrictedfunctionsmall{\mathfrak{a}}{ \folvars{\mathcal{L}} - \mleft\{ x, y \mright\} } \union \mleft\{ \orderedpair{y}{e_{1}} \mright\}
        \union \mleft\{ \orderedpair{x}{e_{0}} \mright\}
    \end{equation}
    It should be clear from \eqref{eq:definition-of-a-modified-x-e0-y-e1-fully-expanded-form} and \eqref{eq:definition-of-a-modified-y-e1-x-e0-fully-expanded-form} that
    \( \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{ \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{\mathfrak{a}}{x}{e_{0}} }{y}{e_{1}} =
        \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{ \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{\mathfrak{a}}{y}{e_{1}} }{x}{e_{0}}\).
\end{proof}

\begin{dfn}
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:first-order-language-interpretation-of-terms}
    If \( t \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-term and \( \mathfrak{a} \) is a variable assignment function of \( \mathcal{L} \) under some \( \mathcal{L} \)-structure \( \mathfrak{A} \),
    then an \keyword{interpretation} of \( t \) in \( \mathfrak{A} \) with \( \mathfrak{a} \)
    (denoted as \folterminterpretation{t}{\mathfrak{A}}{\mathfrak{a}}) is defined as follows:
    \begin{enumerate}[topsep=2pt,label=\textup{\texttt{(\alph*)}}]
        \item If \( t \) consists of a single \( \mathcal{L} \)-variable \( x \), then \( \folterminterpretation{t}{\mathfrak{A}}{\mathfrak{a}} = \mathfrak{a}(x) \)
        \item If \( t \) has the form \( F^{\mathcal{L}}_{i}(\tau) \) for some \( i \in \dom F^{\mathcal{L}} \) and \( \tau \) is a tuple of \( \mathcal{L} \)-terms
            such that \( \dom \tau = \Phi^{\mathcal{L}}(i) \),
            then \( \folterminterpretation{t}{\mathfrak{A}}{\mathfrak{a}} = 
            F^{\mathfrak{A}}_{i} \sequence{\folterminterpretation{\tau_{k}}{\mathfrak{A}}{\mathfrak{a}}}{k \in \dom \tau}\)
    \end{enumerate}
\end{dfn}

\begin{dfn}[Satisfaction]
    \phantomsection \label{dfn:first-order-language-satisfaction-of-formulas}
    Let \( \varphi \) be an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula and \( \mathfrak{a} \) be a \aref{dfn:first-order-language-variable-assignment-function}{variable assignment function}
    of \( \mathcal{L} \) under an \( \mathcal{L} \)-structure \( \mathfrak{A} \).
    We now define precisely what it means for \( \mathfrak{A} \) to satisfy \( \varphi \) with \( \mathfrak{a} \)
    (denoted as \folwffsatisfaction{\mathfrak{A}}{\varphi}{\mathfrak{a}}):
    \begin{enumerate}[topsep=2pt,label=\textup{\texttt{(\alph*)}}]
        \item If \( \varphi \) is an atom of the form \( t_{0} = t_{1} \) where \( t_{0} \) and \( t_{1} \) are \( \mathcal{L} \)-terms,
            then \( \folwffsatisfaction{\mathfrak{A}}{\varphi}{\mathfrak{a}} \) if and only if
            \( \folterminterpretation{t_{0}}{\mathfrak{A}}{\mathfrak{a}} = \folterminterpretation{t_{1}}{\mathfrak{A}}{\mathfrak{a}} \)
        \item If \( \varphi \) is an atom of the form \( P^{\mathcal{L}}_{i}(\tau) \) for some \( i \in \dom P^{\mathcal{L}} \), then
            \( \folwffsatisfaction{\mathfrak{A}}{\varphi}{\mathfrak{a}} \) if and only if
            \( \sequence{ \folterminterpretation{\tau_{j}}{\mathfrak{A}}{\mathfrak{a}} }{j \in \dom \tau} \in P^{\mathfrak{A}}_{i} \)
        \item If \( \varphi \) has the form \( \mleft( \psi \mright) \) or \( \mleft[ \,\psi\, \mright] \) where \( \psi \) is another \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula,
            then \( \folwffsatisfaction{\mathfrak{A}}{\varphi}{\mathfrak{a}} \) if and only if \( \folwffsatisfaction{\mathfrak{A}}{\psi}{\mathfrak{a}} \)
        \item If \( \varphi \) has the form \( \lnot \, \psi \) where \( \psi \) is another \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula, then
            \( \folwffsatisfaction{\mathfrak{A}}{\varphi}{\mathfrak{a}} \) if and only if \( \folwffnonsatisfaction{\mathfrak{A}}{\psi}{\mathfrak{a}} \)
        \item If \( \varphi \) has the form \( \psi \,\,{\land}\,\, \phi \) where \( \psi \) and \( \phi \) are \( \mathcal{L} \)-formulas,
            then \( \folwffsatisfaction{\mathfrak{A}}{\varphi}{\mathfrak{a}} \) if and only if \( \folwffsatisfaction{\mathfrak{A}}{\psi}{\mathfrak{a}} \)
            and \( \folwffsatisfaction{\mathfrak{A}}{\phi}{\mathfrak{a}} \)
        \item If \( \varphi \) has the form \( \psi \,\,{\lor}\,\, \phi \) where \( \psi \) and \( \phi \) are \( \mathcal{L} \)-formulas,
            then \( \folwffsatisfaction{\mathfrak{A}}{\varphi}{\mathfrak{a}} \) if and only if \( \folwffsatisfaction{\mathfrak{A}}{\psi}{\mathfrak{a}} \)
            or \( \folwffsatisfaction{\mathfrak{A}}{\phi}{\mathfrak{a}} \)
        \item If \( \varphi \) has the form \( \forall v \, \psi \) where \( v \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-variable and \( \psi \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula,
            then \( \folwffsatisfaction{\mathfrak{A}}{\varphi}{\mathfrak{a}} \) if and only if
            \( \folwffsatisfaction{\mathfrak{A}}{\psi}{ \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{\mathfrak{a}}{v}{e} } \) for all \( e \in \abs{\mathfrak{A}} \)
        \item If \( \varphi \) has the form \( \exists v \, \psi \) where \( v \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-variable and \( \psi \) is an \( \mathcal{L} \)-formula,
            then \( \folwffsatisfaction{\mathfrak{A}}{\varphi}{\mathfrak{a}} \) if and only if there exists an \( e \in \abs{\mathfrak{A}} \) such that
            \( \folwffsatisfaction{\mathfrak{A}}{\psi}{ \folmodifiedvariableassignmentfunction{\mathfrak{a}}{v}{e} } \)
    \end{enumerate}
    The tuple \( \tuple{\mathfrak{A}, \mathfrak{a}} \) is called an \keyword{interpretation} of \( \varphi \) since it contains all the information needed to
    interpret \( \varphi \).
\end{dfn}

\begin{notation}
    If \( \varphi \) has only one free variable \( x \) and \( e \) denotes an element of the universe of an \( \mathcal{L} \)-structure \( \mathfrak{A} \),
    then we sometimes write \( \varphi(e) \) to express the fact that \( \folwffsatisfaction{\mathfrak{A}}{\varphi}{\mathfrak{a}} \) for all variable assignment
    function \( \mathfrak{a} \) such that \( \mathfrak{a}(x) = e \). If \( \varphi \) has more than one free variable, then a more elaborate notation will be used.
    For instance, if \( \varphi \) has free variables \( x \) and \( y \), then we write \( \varphi(x = e_{0}, y = e_{1}) \) to mean
    \( \folwffsatisfaction{\mathfrak{A}}{\varphi}{\mathfrak{a}} \) for all variable assignment function \( \mathfrak{a} \) such that
    \( \mathfrak{a}(x) = e_{0} \) and \( \mathfrak{a}(y) = e_{1} \).
    If an ``order'' is implicitly associated with the free variables \( x \) and \( y \), then we can abbreviate
    \( \varphi(x = e_{0}, y = e_{1}) \) with \( \varphi(e_{0}, e_{1}) \).
\end{notation}

\begin{thm}
    \phantomsection \label{thm:first-order-language-different-variable-assignment-functions-yield-the-same-interpretation-if-they-agree-for-all-variable-symbols-in-a-term}
    Let \( t \) be an \( \mathcal{L} \)-term and \( \mathfrak{A} \) be an \( \mathcal{L} \)-structure.
    If \( \mathfrak{a}_{0} \) and \( \mathfrak{a}_{1} \) are \aref{dfn:first-order-language-variable-assignment-function}{variable assignment functions}
    of \( \mathcal{L} \) under \( \mathfrak{A} \) such that
    \( \restrictedfunctionsmall{\mathfrak{a}_{0}}{\folvars{t}} = \restrictedfunctionsmall{\mathfrak{a}_{1}}{\folvars{t}} \),
    then \( \folterminterpretation{t}{\mathfrak{A}}{\mathfrak{a}_{0}} = \folterminterpretation{t}{\mathfrak{A}}{\mathfrak{a}_{1}} \).
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
    Since every \( \mathcal{L} \)-term has a finite \aref{dfn:first-order-language-complexity-of-terms}{complexity}, we can use an inductive proof.
    We begin by defining the statement \( P(n) \) as follows: for all \( \mathcal{L} \)-term \( t \) with \( \folcomplexityofterm{t} = n \),
    \( \folterminterpretation{t}{\mathfrak{A}}{\mathfrak{a}_{0}} = \folterminterpretation{t}{\mathfrak{A}}{\mathfrak{a}_{1}} \) holds.
    We will now state the induction hypothesis for an arbitrary \( n \in \naturalset \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    \underline{\scshape Induction Hypothesis:}
    \( P(k) \) holds for all \( k < n \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    Let \( t \) be any \( \mathcal{L} \)-term such that \( \folcomplexityofterm{t} = n \).
    Since \( n \in \naturalset \), either \( n = 0 \) or \( n \neq 0 \).
    We have to show that \( \folterminterpretation{t}{\mathfrak{A}}{\mathfrak{a}_{0}} = \folterminterpretation{t}{\mathfrak{A}}{\mathfrak{a}_{1}} \) is implied in both cases.

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    {\scshape Case} \( n = 0 \):
    Clearly \( t \) consists of either a single \( \mathcal{L} \)-variable or a 0-ary function symbol.
    If \( t \) consists of the variable symbol \( x \), then we have \( \folterminterpretation{t}{\mathfrak{A}}{\mathfrak{a}_{0}} = \mathfrak{a}_{0}(x) \)
    and \( \folterminterpretation{t}{\mathfrak{A}}{\mathfrak{a}_{1}} = \mathfrak{a}_{1}(x) \) by \cref{dfn:first-order-language-interpretation-of-terms}.
    Since \( x \in \folvars{t} \), necessarily \( \mathfrak{a}_{0}(x) = \mathfrak{a}_{1}(x) \) by our initial assumption.
    Thus, \( \folterminterpretation{t}{\mathfrak{A}}{\mathfrak{a}_{0}} = \folterminterpretation{t}{\mathfrak{A}}{\mathfrak{a}_{1}} \).
    If \( t \) consists of a 0-ary function symbol \( F^{\mathcal{L}}_{i}() \), then \cref{dfn:first-order-language-interpretation-of-terms}
    requires that \( \folterminterpretation{t}{\mathfrak{A}}{\mathfrak{a}_{0}} = F^{\mathfrak{A}}_{i}() = \folterminterpretation{t}{\mathfrak{A}}{\mathfrak{a}_{1}} \).

    \vspace{0.3cm}
    \noindent
    {\scshape Case} \( n \neq 0 \):
    It follows from \cref{cor:first-order-language-terms-with-non-zero-complexity-must-have-a-parent-function-symbol} that
    \( t \) has the form \( F^{\mathcal{L}}_{i}(\tau) \) for some \( i \in \dom F^{\mathcal{L}} \) where \( \tau \) is a tuple of \( \mathcal{L} \)-terms
    and \( \abs{\tau} = \Phi^{L}(i) \). Thus we have by \cref{dfn:first-order-language-interpretation-of-terms}
    \begin{align}
        \folterminterpretation{t}{\mathfrak{A}}{\mathfrak{a}_{0}}
        &= F^{\mathfrak{A}}_{i} \sequence{ \folterminterpretation{\tau_{j}}{\mathfrak{A}}{\mathfrak{a}_{0}} }{j \in \dom \tau} \phantomsection \label{eq:interpret-with-delta0}\\
        \folterminterpretation{t}{\mathfrak{A}}{\mathfrak{a}_{1}}
        &= F^{\mathfrak{A}}_{i} \sequence{ \folterminterpretation{\tau_{j}}{\mathfrak{A}}{\mathfrak{a}_{1}} }{j \in \dom \tau} \phantomsection \label{eq:interpret-with-delta1}
    \end{align}
    Let \( m \in \dom \tau \) be arbitrary. It follows then that \( \tau_{m} \) is a \aref{dfn:first-order-language-subterms}{subterm} of \( t \); thus,
    \cref{cor:first-order-language-the-complexity-of-a-subterm-is-less-than-the-complexity-of-the-parent-term} guarantees that \( \folcomplexityofterm{\tau_{m}} < n \).
    By the induction hypothesis, we further deduce that
    \( \folterminterpretation{\tau_{m}}{\mathfrak{A}}{\mathfrak{a}_{0}} = \folterminterpretation{\tau_{m}}{\mathfrak{A}}{\mathfrak{a}_{1}} \).
    This means that the tuples in \eqref{eq:interpret-with-delta0} and \eqref{eq:interpret-with-delta1} are equal and that
